Let me check the environment: is Rocq installed? What version?

Let me plan the proof.

We have locally nameless STLC with CBV semantics. Need strong normalization for closed well-typed terms.

Standard approach: logical relation indexed by type, on closed terms:
- `LR Bool t := strongly_normalizing t` (well, for CBV, maybe SN is enough for bool; actually need value-ish? Let's think)
- `LR (T1 -> T2) t := strongly_normalizing t /\ forall t', LR T1 t' -> LR T2 (t t')` — for CBV, we need that the argument is a value? In CBV, application `t1 t2` steps only when t2 is a value (ST_App2 requires v1 value, and reduces t2; ST_App1 reduces t1; ST_AppAbs requires t1 to be abs and v value). Hmm, actually in this semantics, `tm_app v1 t2` with v1 a value that's not an abs: if v1 is true/false then no step at all. Well-typedness prevents that (applications are typed with function type), but at the logical relation level we quantify over all closed t1 of arrow type. Since LR arrow implies SN, and typed closed terms... Hmm.

Let's design the relation carefully following the standard "Tait's method for CBV" (e.g., from Software Foundations / or the "locally nameless" tutorial by Charguéraud? Actually this file resembles the "STLC" benchmark from a set of normalization benchmarks — maybe from "Logical relations" exercises).

Standard approach in SF (StlcProp / Norm): The relation is defined as:
```
Fixpoint R T :=
  match T with
  | Bool => fun t => strongly_normalizing t /\ (t = true \/ t = false)?? 
```
Hmm, in SF's normalization chapter for CBV STLC, they use:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Bool => fun t => strongly_normalizing t /\ value t? 
```
Actually in SF `Norm.v`, the relation for CBV is defined as:
- R_Bool t := value t /\ strongly_normalizing t? Hmm let me recall. In SF's `Stlc` normalization (`Norm.v`), they prove weak normalization via a "step-indexed" approach with `halts`. For CBV they define:
```
Fixpoint R (T:ty) (t:tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | TBool => halts t /\ value t  (or just halts t?)
  | TArrow T1 T2 => halts t /\ value t /\ forall t', R T1 t' -> R T2 (tapp t t')
```
Hmm, but actually for CBV, strong normalization is easier with the relation `SN t /\ (value t -> ...)`. Let me think about what's needed.

Key issue: ST_App2 requires `value v1` before reducing argument. And ST_AppAbs requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T t)` and `value v`. Also ST_App1 requires `locally_closed t2`.

If we define the logical relation on closed terms only (locally_closed t), then:
- ST_App1: `t1 t2 --> t1' t2` if `t1 --> t1'` and `lc t2`. Fine since t2 closed.
- ST_App2: `v1 t2 --> v1 t2'` if `value v1`, `t2 --> t2'`. Needs `value v1` and v1 closed implicitly (value includes lc for abs).
- ST_AppAbs: `(abs T t) v --> open t v` requires lc(abs T t) and value v.

Now, to prove strong normalization of a well-typed closed term `t`, we'd want:
1. Fundamental theorem: if Gamma |-- t \in T and Gamma is a "good" context (maps x to T with the substitution value satisfying R T), then R T t[sigma] essentially. Working with locally nameless and fvar contexts.

Since the terms are locally nameless with fvar atoms, we need contexts Gamma : atom -> option ty, and a substitution sigma : atom -> tm mapping variables to closed terms. Then t[sigma] would require a substitution function. Alternatively, we can define the interpretation of contexts directly: `Gamma |= sigma` means for all x T, Gamma x = Some T -> LR T (sigma x), and prove:

  If Gamma |-- t \in T, lc (subst sigma t), and Gamma |= sigma, then LR T (subst sigma t).

Hmm, but note that in the T_Abs case, we need to open the body with a fresh fvar x, then extend sigma with x |-> some closed value. Standard locally nameless approach: use cofinite quantification.

Alternatively, we can avoid substitution altogether by working with a relation on open terms with environments... Actually, the standard Charguéraud-style locally nameless proof: define `body` relation etc. But here no body relation is given; we can define it.

Alternative simpler approach: Since the context is arbitrary atoms, maybe we can prove the fundamental theorem with sigma mapping atoms to terms that are in LR. We need a substitution function `subst` (atom -> tm) -> tm -> tm. But careful: T_Abs's premise uses `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` for fresh x; so the body opens with an fvar. When we apply the IH, we substitute all fvars including x with sigma x. So we need the IH statement for terms open with fvars.

Let me define:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```

Note: this is a "parallel" substitution for free variables only; bvars are untouched, appropriate since locally nameless bodies are opened with fvar.

We need: if `locally_closed t` and sigma maps to closed terms, then `locally_closed (subst sigma t)`. Hmm, actually lc of subst requires lc of each sigma x used. Alternatively, we can state the fundamental theorem with lc as a hypothesis.

Actually, for the application case: `has_type Gamma t1 (T1 -> T2)`, `has_type Gamma t2 T1`. IH gives LR (T1->T2) (subst sigma t1) and LR T1 (subst sigma t2). Need LR T2 (app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)). By definition of LR arrow: LR T2 (app u v) for LR-arrow u and LR T1 v. That's exactly a component of the arrow relation.

But wait: we need to show LR T2 (app u v) where u ranges over LR arrow terms. In the arrow definition we can directly include `forall v, LR T1 v -> LR T2 (tm_app u v)` as a conjunct. But for the abstraction case, `u = tm_abs T1 body` needs to satisfy that. When we apply `u` to `v`, `u` is an abstraction (a value) so ST_AppAbs applies... but only if `locally_closed (tm_abs T1 body)` and `value v`. `value v` — do we have that from LR T1 v? We need the LR to imply `value v` for all types! For CBV, the crucial property is that the logical relation implies value (at least for closed terms). Let's define:

```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t /\ (value t)  maybe?
```

Hmm, but do we need `value t` for Bool? For the arrow case we need: to prove `LR (T1->T2) (tm_abs T1 body)`, we need SN of the abs (true since abs with closed body is a value, and values are SN — need to show values are SN: a closed value has no steps? Abs is closed, and step on abs? No step rules for abs itself, true, false. So any value is SN trivially... well, need value implies SN: induction, no step from a value. Do we have `value t -> forall t', t --> t' -> False`? Let's check: value (abs) — can abs step? No rule produces `tm_abs --> t'`. value true/false — no rules. So yes, value implies no steps.)

For LR arrow: `forall v, LR T1 v -> LR T2 (app t v)`. Given `t = abs T1' body` closed, and `LR T1 v` gives `value v` (needed for ST_AppAbs) — we need value v from LR T1 v for arbitrary T1. So yes, LR must include value. Then `app (abs T1' body) v --> open body v`. We need `LR T2 (open body v)`.

But wait, the type annotation in the abstraction: `tm_abs T1' body` typed at `T1 -> T2` means T1' = T1 by inversion. Fine.

To prove `LR T2 (open body v)` we use the fundamental theorem: from `has_type (x |-> T1; Gamma) (open body (fvar x)) T2` for fresh x, we get `LR T2 (subst sigma' (open body (fvar x)))` where sigma' maps x to v and other atoms according to sigma. Need a lemma: if x not in fvars of body (and other conditions), then subst (x |-> v; sigma) (open body (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma body) v.

Hmm, this is the classic locally nameless substitution lemma. To avoid pain, note that `open_rec` replaces bvar 0 with u under binders (increasing k), and subst replaces fvars. The interaction lemma: `subst sigma (open u v)` ... Actually we need:

`subst (extend x v sigma) (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t) v` provided `x \notin fv t` and `lc v` maybe. Let's think about it more carefully.

Hmm, actually maybe there's a simpler approach that avoids substitution lemmas entirely: instead of substituting free variables with closed terms, we can define the context interpretation where each variable is mapped to a *value* in LR, and prove the fundamental theorem directly on the term with free variables, where the "semantic" relation is defined on terms with free variables? But the LR is about closed terms and strong normalization of terms with free variables doesn't make sense unless we consider fvars as stuck.

Alternative: define LR over terms possibly with free variables, where fvars are treated as values (opaque constants). For T_Abs: need LR (T1->T2) (abs T1 body). Components:

   - SN (app (abs T1 body) v): we know abs body is SN, v is SN (since LR implies SN), and for CBV, app of SN terms is SN? Not in general! For CBV, if both t1 and t2 are SN, is app t1 t2 SN? Since reduction of app is: reduce t1 (while t2 fixed, requires lc t2), reduce t2 once t1 is a value (requires value t1), and beta when t1 is abs and t2 value. Since t1 SN and t2 SN, the reductions are finite: reduce t1 finitely many steps to a normal form; then either t1 is not a value and stuck (app stuck, SN) or t1 is a value: if t1 is true/false, stuck; if abs, then reduce t2 finitely many steps until... hmm, when t1 is an abs, ST_App2 applies only when t2 steps, and ST_AppAbs applies when t2 is a value. If t2 is SN, along any reduction sequence t2 eventually gets stuck at a normal form which is either a value (abs/true/false) or a stuck term (e.g., fvar-app, or app of non-value normal forms). If t2 reduces to a value, then beta step, then we need SN of open body v, which needs the hypothesis LR T2 (open body v)... wait, we're trying to prove LR T2 (app (abs T1 body) v) = SN (app ...) /\ ... " Hmm, we need a lemma: if t1 is SN, t2 is SN, and for every v such that t2 -->* v and value v, open body v is SN (this is the "substitution" property), then app t1 t2 is SN. Alternative approach: The classic Tait/Girard proof for CBV uses the LR where the arrow case includes SN and value, and then proves:
- If t1 is SN and t2 is SN and for all v with value v, t2 -->* v -> body open ... hmm.

Actually, the cleanest formulation for CBV strong normalization: define a "reducibility" relation and use the standard three lemmas:
1. CR1: LR T t -> SN t.
2. CR2: LR T t -> (t --> t') -> LR T t'. (closure under reduction, "reducibility is closed under expansion/backward reduction" — here forward reduction).
3. CR3: neutral terms (stuck terms) of type T whose immediate reducts are all in LR T are in LR T.

For CBV, we also need LR implies value for the "value" part, and the app lemma requires a "strong normalization of application" lemma.

Hmm, let me seek a simpler path: Since strong normalization is what we want, perhaps define LR differently: `LR T t := SN t /\ value t /\ ...`. For the SN part of the app case, we can use a general lemma about SN of applications:

Lemma `sn_app`: if SN t1, SN t2, and for all t1' t2', t1 -->* t1' -> SN t1' -> t2 -->* t2' -> value t2' -> (either t1' is an abs and ...) then SN (app t1 t2).

Standard approach: define SN via well-founded induction on the multiset? Or use the "Acc" characterization: `strongly_normalizing t` is literally `Acc step t` but defined inductively as SN_intro = Acc. Actually `strongly_normalizing` as defined is exactly `Acc step t`:

```
Inductive Acc (R) x := Acc_intro : (forall y, R x y -> Acc R y) -> Acc R x.
```
Indeed SN_intro matches Acc_intro. So `strongly_normalizing t` is `Acc step t` definitionally (same constructor shape). We can prove equivalence: `strongly_normalizing t <-> Acc step t`. Actually just by induction/case analysis, they're isomorphic; define `SN t := strongly_normalizing t` and prove inversion principles.

To prove SN of app, the standard method: use the well-founded induction on `(SN t1, SN t2)` with lexicographic ordering, or the "SN of a term with respects to a relation" approach: define the relation on pairs... A cleaner method: prove closure properties:
- If SN t then SN t' for t --> t' (immediate from inversion).

Actually, let me use a different well-known technique: prove SN via a "measure" using `SN` as an inductive predicate and a "combine" lemma for the application construct. There's a standard result: for CBV with values being abs/true/false, define the "candidate" relation on type as:

R_Bool = { t | SN t /\ value t }? Hmm, no: true/false are values; a well-typed Bool term is SN and reduces to true or false, but not necessarily a value itself. Standard approach: prove `SN (app (abs T t) v)` when:
- `locally_closed (abs T t)`,

Circular-ish but can be handled by well-founded induction on SN-derivation of v.

Let's define the key lemma:

**Lemma sn_app_abs**: for all T t v, locally_closed (abs T t) -> SN v -> (forall v', v -->* v' -> value v' -> SN (open t v')) -> SN (app (abs T t) v).

Hmm, proving this requires simultaneous reasoning. Proof by induction on SN v (i.e., Acc step v). Take v with SN; for all reducts v' of v, SN v'. Goal: SN (app abs v). Use SN_intro: for any u with app abs v --> u, show SN u.
Cases:
- ST_App1: app abs v --> app t1' v requires abs --> t1', impossible since abs can't step. (Need lemma: value t -> no step.)
- ST_App2: app abs v --> app abs v' with v --> v'. Need SN (app abs v'): apply IH on v' (from SN v inversion). But IH requires the "forall v'' reachable from v', value v'' -> SN (open t v'')" condition, which we get from the hypothesis by transitivity of multi. Good.
- ST_AppAbs: app abs v --> open t v with value v. Need SN (open t v): from the hypothesis with v -->* v (reflexivity + value v).

Great, this works! And it's by induction on SN v (Acc induction). But wait: the IH in the Acc induction is `forall y, v --> y -> SN y ->? ...` hmm. Let's set up: prove by induction on `SN v` the statement `forall t T, lc (abs T t) -> (forall v', multi step v v' -> value v' -> SN (open t v')) -> SN (app (abs T t) v)`. In the ST_App2 case, we need the IH at v' where v --> v', and SN v' is given by the SN_intro inversion of SN v. And the hypothesis "forall v'' multi v' v'' ..." follows from multi transitivity: v -->* v'' from v --> v' -->* v''.

Wait, but does the IH statement need to be generalized over t and T? Yes, for the ST_App2 case we apply IH with the same t T. Fine.

And in the ST_AppAbs case, `open t v` uses the same v, and the hypothesis gives SN (open t v) since multi_refl.

Also ST_App1 case: need `abs T t --> t1'` impossible. Prove lemma `value_no_step`.

Hmm, one subtlety: ST_App1 has a side condition `locally_closed t2`, and t2 = v; but we don't care since no step from abs.

Does R imply value? R_Bool t := SN t. Then for T1 = Bool, R Bool v' doesn't imply value v'. But we need value v' to know SN (open body v') from the hypothesis... If v' is SN and not a value and normal (stuck), then app abs v' is also stuck (ST_App2 requires v --> v', none; ST_AppAbs requires value v'). So it's SN without needing the body.

Hmm, so the hypothesis for non-value v' isn't needed. But how do we structure the proof to handle "value v'" vs not? In the Acc induction, to prove SN (app abs v), we consider all steps from app abs v. ST_AppAbs gives open t v and requires value v — so the hypothesis gives it. ST_App2 gives app abs v' with v --> v'; apply IH. So indeed in the SN proof, we only need the hypothesis at value-reducts. Great, so the lemma as stated works: `(forall v', multi step v v' -> value v' -> SN (open t v'))`.

Wait, one more: ST_App1 with t1 = abs T t: `t1 --> t1'` — impossible. Good.

Now, in the abstraction case of the fundamental theorem, we need to show:
For `u = tm_abs T1 body` (where body = subst sigma t1, with the abs itself closed):
 (a) SN u — because u is a value with lc; value => no steps => SN.
 (b) forall v, R T1 v -> R T2 (app u v).

For (b), we need to show R T2 (app u v). Structure depends on T2:
- If T2 = Bool: need SN (app u v). Use `sn_app_abs` with hypothesis from fundamental theorem: for v' value with v -->* v', need SN (open body v'). We have from the fundamental theorem at the body: `R T2 (open body v')` — since `xc |-> T1; Gamma |-- open t1 (fvar xc) \in T2` holds for fresh xc, and the context interpretation extended with xc |-> v' holds (since R T1 v'), we get R T2 (subst sigma' ...) = R T2 (open body v') given the substitution lemma. Then R T2 implies SN. 

- If T2 = T2a -> T2b: need SN (app u v) plus forall w, R T2a w -> R T2b (app (app u v) w). The latter: we need R T2b ((u v) w). We have R (T2a -> T2b) (open body v) from fundamental theorem, which gives the application clause: R T2b (app (open body v) w). But we need R T2b (app (app u v) w) — and `app (app u v) w` vs `app (open body v) w`. Is `app (open body v) w` related to `app (app u v) w`? They're different terms unless... we could use closure of R under reduction/expansion: `app u v --> open body v` (beta). Then `app (app u v) w --> app (open body v) w` by ST_App1 (requires lc w). So R T2b (app (open body v) w) plus a "backward closure" property of R: if t --> t' and R t' then R t. That's the standard CR2 property! Then from R T2b (app (open body v) w) and `app (app u v) w --> app (open body v) w`, we'd get R T2b (app (app u v) w) by backward closure.

Alternatively, we could define R arrow differently to make this easier... Hmm, but backward closure seems necessary anyway. Actually a cleaner way: prove in general that R T is closed under *expansion* (if t --> t' and R T t' then R T t) as part of the fundamental properties (CR2). This is standard.

So the whole proof architecture:
1. Define SN-related basic lemmas (value no step; SN of values; inversion of SN).
2. Define `R : ty -> tm -> Prop`:
   ```
   Fixpoint R T t :=
     match T with
     | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
     | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => strongly_normalizing t /\ (forall v, R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
     end.
   ```
   (No value component! But then how do we handle ST_AppAbs needing value v? Ah — the SN-app lemma handles it via the "value v'" hypothesis. But wait: in the arrow abstraction proof, the third component for abs u: forall v, R T1 v -> R T2 (app u v). To prove SN (app u v) by sn_app_abs we need `forall v', v -->* v' -> value v' -> SN (open body v')`. We have that from the fundamental theorem at the body plus R implies SN. So value comes in as a hypothesis in the lemma, not from R.

Plan A (add lc to R):
```
Fixpoint R T t :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t

                      (forall v, R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.
```
Properties:
- R T t -> lc t (projection, by induction on T; for arrow it's direct via triple conjunction; need to structure so projection works: define as nested conjunction or as a record; simplest:
```
Definition R T t := locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\ 
   match T with Ty_Bool => True | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => forall v, R T1 v -> R T2 (app t v) end.
```
Using a Fixpoint with match returning a function is nicer:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
       (forall v, R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.
```

- R T t -> lc t: destruct T; simpl; tauto-ish.
- R T t -> SN t: same.
- CR2 (forward): R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'.
  - Bool: lc preserved by step (lemma step_preserves_lc), SN from SN inversion.
  - Arrow: lc preserved; SN; app clause: given R T1 w, need R T2 (app t' w). We have R T2 (app t w). And app t w --> app t' w (ST_App1 needs lc w — available from R T1 w!).   - Bool: lc of t from lc t' and step preservation backward? t --> t' means t' is a reduct; need lc t from lc t'. Hmm, that's preservation backwards: if t --> t' and lc t' then lc t? Is that true? Now the harder part: the substitution/open interaction lemma. In the T_Abs case, the typing premise is about `open t1 (fvar x)`. We need to relate `subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))` with `open (subst sigma t1) v`, where sigma' = extend x v sigma, and x fresh w.r.t. t1.

Let's define substitution:
```
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```

Wait, but this naive substitution is wrong under binders? No—it's fine for fvar-only substitution since binders only bind bvars. fvars are never bound. So `subst sigma (abs T t1) = abs T (subst sigma t1)` is correct.

Actually, there's a neat trick: since `strongly_normalizing` is `Acc step`, we have well-founded induction. For the app case, we can do:

Prove: forall v, SN v -> lc v -> forall u, SN u -> (compat condition) -> SN (app u v).

Induction on SN u? Actually both are needed. Let's think about what reduction paths look like: 
app u v --> app u' v (u --> u') [requires lc v]
app u v --> app u v' (v --> v') [requires value u]
app u v --> open body v [u = abs T body, value v, lc abs]

The standard proof: do induction on SN u and SN v simultaneously (lexicographic isn't right since steps interleave). The clean approach is to use the general lemma:

"SN is closed under the rule: if for all u', v' with u -->* u', v -->* v' and (both values) beta is SN ..."

Alternative: **Use `SN` defined via `Acc` and prove the application lemma with a well-founded induction on the multiset {SN u, SN v}** using the standard "SN of a term formed by application" technique:

Standard technique (from e.g. the "strong normalization for STLC" proofs with call-by-value): Define `SN t` and prove:
1. If SN t and t --> t' then SN t'.
2. If all immediate reducts of t are SN then SN t.
3. (Application) If v is a value with SN v and body... 

Actually, here's a cleaner way to organize the app lemma. Consider the relation between `app u v` and the pair. We want to prove `SN (app u v)` by induction on `SN u` (Acc on step for u) and `SN v`. In the inductive step, we need to show all immediate reducts of app u v are SN:
- ST_App1: u --> u', then app u v --> app u' v. Need SN (app u' v). Apply the *outer* induction with u' (SN u' from SN u ✓) and same v: IH at u' requires the compatibility condition for u' — which follows from that of u restricted to reducts u' -->* u''. But the outer induction hypothesis at u' is available only if our lemma statement's IH is strong enough: we do induction on SN u with the statement `forall v, SN v -> lc v -> compat u v -> SN (app u v)`. The IH at u' can be applied with any v (SN v) and compat u' v (derived from compat u v). So we don't need induction on v for this case. ✓
- ST_App2: value u, v --> v'. Need SN (app u v'). Apply... the IH at u requires... hmm, u is fixed here, v' is a reduct so SN v' from SN v ✓, but we need SN (app u v'), which is not the outer IH (that's at u' with u --> u'). We need an inner induction on SN v: `forall u, value u -> SN u -> compat u v -> SN (app u v)`. Hmm, but that's essentially the same lemma at fixed u.

So the structure: primary induction on SN u? Let's see: at ST_App2 we need the result at the same u but v' and value u. So we need a secondary induction on SN v, at least for value u. Restructure:

Lemma: `forall u, SN u -> forall v, SN v -> lc v -> compat u v -> SN (app u v)`.
Proof by induction on SN u.
- For the given u and v: we need to show SN (app u v) by showing all reducts SN.
  - ST_App1: u --> u', use IH (SN u') with same v: needs compat u' v.
  - ST_App2: requires value u. Then v --> v'; need SN (app u v'). Here we can't use IH (it's about reducts of u, and u has none since value u... wait, actually if value u then u has no steps, so SN u is "trivial"; we could use an inner induction on SN v). So do a nested induction on SN v when value u:
    
    Inner lemma: forall v, SN v -> lc v -> (value u) -> SN u -> compat u v -> SN (app u v). Proof by induction on SN v:
    - Reducts of app u v:
      - ST_App1: u --> u' — impossible since value u (no steps).
      - ST_App2: v --> v', use IH at v' (SN v'), lc v' (from lc v and step lc preservation), value u, compat u v'.

    ✓.

The inner lemma is a separate lemma parameterized by u. Hmm, dependency: outer lemma needs inner lemma; inner lemma is proven independently (by induction on SN v) with assumptions value u, SN u (not actually needed except maybe lc u?), lc u? For ST_App1 we need lc u? No—ST_App1 is about u stepping; if value u, impossible. For ST_AppAbs we need lc (abs T body) = lc u and value v.
    
    So inner lemma: forall u, value u -> lc u -> SN u (redundant) -> forall v, SN v -> lc v -> compat u v -> SN (app u v). Proof by induction on SN v. ✓ (No dependency on outer.)
    
    Outer lemma: forall u, SN u -> forall v, SN v -> lc v -> compat u v -> SN (app u v). Proof by induction on SN u. Reducts:
    - ST_App1 (u --> u'): IH at u' with v: need SN u' ✓, SN v ✓, lc v ✓, compat u' v ✓.
    - ST_App2 (value u, v --> v'): use inner lemma at u with value u, lc u (need lc u! do we have it? In the outer lemma we don't assume lc u. Hmm. But value u — does value imply lc? v_abs requires lc; v_true/v_false are lc. So value u -> lc u ✓ (prove lemma). And SN u ✓. Then inner lemma at v' with SN v' ✓, lc v' (from lc v and step lc preservation), compat u v' ✓.
    - ST_AppAbs: u = abs T body, lc u ✓, value v ✓, result open body v: compat u v gives SN ✓ (reflexive multis).
    ✓.

Wait, but in the outer lemma, case ST_App2: the rule requires `value v1` where v1 = u; and t2 --> t2'. Yes.

But hold on: in the outer lemma, when proving SN (app u v) by SN_intro, we need to handle all reducts. ST_App1 requires lc t2 = lc v ✓ (assumed). ST_App2 requires value u. ST_AppAbs requires lc (abs T body) and value v. Fine.

And the compat condition: `compat u v := forall u' v', u -->* u' -> value u' -> v -->* v' -> value v' -> SN (open body v')` where u' must be an abs. Now back to the main relation design. Let me reconsider the "V/R" design.

Define:
```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => value t /\ (forall v, V T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
  end
with R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
  (forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V T v).
```
Mutual fixpoint. Hmm, but mutual fixpoints with Prop are fine in Coq (Fixpoint ... with ...). But is this well-founded structurally? R calls V at the same T; V calls R at subterms of T (T2), and V T1. Both are structural on T. We can define them as a mutual Fixpoint, or define V first with a local reference... Since V (T1->T2) refers to R T2 and V T1, and R T refers to V T, a mutual fixpoint works:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fun t => 
      value t /\ (forall v, V T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
  end
with R (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V Ty_Bool v)
  | ... 
```
Hmm, R doesn't need to match on T really: `R T t := locally_closed t /\ SN t /\ forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V T v`. Since V is structural on T and R is called from V only at subterms, this mutual definition is structurally recursive? R's body calls V T (same T), and V's body calls R T2 (subterm). Coq's guard condition should accept a mutual fixpoint where the recursive calls are on subterms, with the "with" clause. Let me think: the mutual fixpoint requires that recursive calls in V's body be on structurally smaller arguments, and R's body can call V on the same T (same argument) — that's allowed? In Coq, for mutual fixpoints, calls between the functions must decrease the argument. `R T` calling `V T` on the same T is not decreasing. Hmm, that might be rejected. 

We can avoid mutual recursion by defining V and R with R not calling V structurally... Alternative: define R as a single Fixpoint and V as a derived definition:
```
Fixpoint R T t :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => lc t /\ SN t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall v, V T1 v -> R T2 (app t v))
  end
where V T v := R T v /\ value v? 
```
Hmm, what should V be? V T v should mean "v is a value and behaves correctly at type T". Perhaps `V T v := value v /\ R T v`. Let's check whether this works.

Define R (Fixpoint on T):
- R Bool t := lc t /\ SN t.
- R (T1->T2) t := lc t /\ SN t /\ (forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app t v)).

This is the earlier definition with `value v` added to the quantification! This is the key change: the arrow clause quantifies only over *value* arguments that are R. Does that fix the earlier problem?

Recall the problem: T_App case needs R T2 (app u v) where R T1 v but v might not be a value. If the arrow clause requires value v, we can't directly apply. Hmm. But now R T1 v (for non-value v) — we'd need to show R T2 (app u v) directly via its definition:
- lc (app u v) ✓ (lc u, lc v from R).
- SN (app u v): via the general app SN lemma (works for arbitrary SN u, v with compat condition).
- If T2 is an arrow: forall w, value w -> R T2a w -> R T2b (app (app u v) w). Hmm, here we need the same for the application term. Since v may not be a value, app u v is stuck unless v steps. We need to prove R T2b (app (app u v) w) where now w IS a value. 

Hmm, let's think. We know R (T1->T2) u, which gives: forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app u v). But v isn't a value. So we can't use it directly. However, since v is SN and lc, either v is a value or v steps. If v steps, then app u v steps (ST_App2 requires value u — but u might not be a value either!). Ugh.

OK here's the thing: for well-typed terms, a closed term of type T is either a value or steps. That's the "progress" property. If we could establish progress for well-typed closed terms, then in the T_App case v: R T1 v would come from the fundamental theorem for a well-typed v, so v is a value or steps... but R is defined semantically without knowledge of typing.

Alternative: include in R a "value or steps" disjunction? Hmm, that's like "neutral" closure. Let's think about what properties we need of R for the T_App case:
Given R (T1->T2) u and R T1 v, prove R T2 (app u v):
1. lc ✓.
2. SN ✓ via app SN lemma with compat.
3. Arrow clause (if T2 = T2a->T2b): forall w value, R T2a w -> R T2b (app (app u v) w).

For (3), consider: we know R (T1->T2) u gives forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app u v). If v is a value, we get R T2 (app u v), and then the arrow clause of R T2 (app u v) gives R T2b (app (app u v) w) ✓. So if v is a value, we're done. If v is not a value... hmm.

What if we add to R T1 v the property "value v ∨ (exists v', v --> v')"? That's not true for stuck terms like `app true true` which are lc and SN. But maybe we can define R such that non-values that are stuck are excluded? R Bool t := lc t ∧ SN t includes `app true true` (stuck but SN). Hmm, should it? `app true true` is ill-typed. If R Bool were restricted to terms that reduce to true/false (halts at a bool value) then stuck terms would be excluded. Let's consider defining:

R T t := lc t ∧ SN t ∧ (forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V T v)  ... with V T v := "v is a value of type T in the semantic sense":
  V Bool v := v = true ∨ v = false

This is the R/V pair from before. Then, does R T1 v imply "v is a value or steps"? No: `app true true` is SN and has no value reducts (it's stuck), so R Bool (app true true) holds vacuously. But does that break the arrow clause case? Let's re-examine with this R:
T_App: R (T1->T2) u, R T1 v, prove R T2 (app u v):
1. lc ✓.
2. SN via app lemma.
3. forall z, app u v -->* z -> value z -> V T2 z. 
   Given a value reduct z = v_z of app u v. How can app u v reduce to a value? Along the way, u reduces to a value u', and v reduces to a value v', and then beta. So z = open body v' where u -->* abs T body, v -->* v' (value). We need V T2 (open body v'). We have V (T1->T2) u' from R (T1->T2) u and the value reduct u' (u -->* u' value u'). V (T1->T2) u' gives: forall w, V T1 w -> R T2 (app u' w). And V T1 v' from R T1 v and v -->* v' value. So R T2 (app u' v'). And app u' v' --> open body v' (ST_AppAbs, needs lc u' ✓ from value, value v' ✓). Since R T2 (app u' v') implies SN (app u' v'), and the beta step gives SN (open body v'), hmm, but we need V T2 (open body v'), which is stronger than SN.

Hmm! Can we derive V T2 (open body v') from R T2 (app u' v')? R T2 (app u' v') = lc ∧ SN ∧ all value-reducts in V T2. A value-reduct of app u' v': the first step is beta to open body v', so any value reduct of app u' v' is a value reduct of open body v' (reachable via multi from open body v'). So V T2 (open body v') does NOT follow directly — the R property is about value reducts, and open body v' itself might be a non-value term that reduces to value z. But we want V T2 z where z is a value reachable from open body v'. R T2 (open body v') would give that. Do we have R T2 (open body v')? We have R T2 (app u' v') and app u' v' --> open body v'. Backward closure (expansion): from R T2 (app u' v') and app u' v' --> open body v', can we derive R T2 (open body v')? No! Backward closure goes the other direction (from R of target to R of source). Forward closure: R T2 (app u' v') and app u' v' --> open body v' gives R T2 (open body v') ✓ — yes, forward closure (CR2)! Because open body v' is a reduct. So if R is closed under reduction (forward), then R T2 (open body v') ✓, giving V T2 z for value reducts z ✓.

So this R works if we prove closure under reduction. Let's check closure under reduction for this R definition:
R T t := lc t ∧ SN t ∧ (forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V T v).
- Forward: t --> t'. lc ✓ (step lc), SN ✓ (SN inversion), and for value reducts v of t': t -->* v via t --> t' -->* v ✓.
✓ Easy (no need for the arrow clause subtleties!). 

This definitional style (R as "all value reducts are V") makes CR2 trivial. 

Now the T_Abs case with this definition:
t = abs T1 body, lc ✓ (need from typing!), SN ✓ (abs doesn't step), and forall v, abs -->* v -> value v -> V (T1->T2) v. Since abs has no steps, abs -->* v implies v = abs. So need V (T1->T2) (abs T1 body) = value abs ✓ ∧ forall w, V T1 w -> R T2 (app (abs T1 body) w).

Now the T_App case: R (T1->T2) u, R T1 v, prove R T2 (app u v):
- lc ✓.

Wait, but we need to carefully do the case analysis on how `app u v -->* z` with z a value. We need a lemma describing reduction sequences of applications. This is the crux. Let's think about the possible reduction sequences from `app u v`:

By the structure of step:
- ST_AppAbs: app (abs T body) v --> open body v when lc abs and value v.
- ST_App1: app t1 t2 --> app t1' t2 when t1 --> t1' and lc t2.
- ST_App2: app v1 t2 --> app v1 t2' when value v1 and t2 --> t2'.

For a multi-step sequence app u v -->* z where z is a value. Since a value can't be an application, z must be reached by the beta rule at the last step (or z = app u v itself, but app u v is not a value since values are abs/true/false). So the last step is ST_AppAbs: z = open body w with the previous term = app (abs T body) w, value w. The prefix app u v -->* app (abs T body) w. Now we need to analyze this prefix. Claim: if app u v -->* app u' v' with u' an abs value, then u -->* u' and (v -->* v' or...). Hmm, careful: the reductions of app u v can interleave: reduce u to an abs, then reduce v, then possibly... once u is an abs and v keeps reducing, ST_App2 applies. But could u reduce again after being an abs? No, abs has no steps. But wait: could u reduce to a *non-abs value* like true? Then app true v: ST_AppAbs doesn't apply (true isn't an abs), ST_App1 doesn't apply (true doesn't step), ST_App2 applies if true is a value (yes) and v steps. So then only v reduces. So in general:

Lemma `app_multi_value`: if app u v -->* w and w is a value, then either:
  (a) w = app u' v' with u -->* u', v -->* v', value (app u' v') — impossible since app isn't a value, or
  (b) there exist T body w' such that u -->* abs T body? Hmm, not exactly: the sequence may reduce v while u is still not a value? No: ST_App2 requires value u. So v can only reduce when u is a value. And u can only reduce when it's not a value (trivially).

Let's think of the reduction sequence as a sequence of steps. Let's prove:
`app u v -->* app u' v'` implies (u -->* u' ∧ v = v') OR (value u ∧ u = u' ∧ v -->* v') OR (exists u'' v'', u -->* u'' ∧ value u'' ∧ u'' = u' ... hmm interleavings).

Actually, the possible sequence: first reduce u some number of steps (via ST_App1) to some u0; then if u0 is a value, reduce v some steps (via ST_App2) to some v0; then beta. But could we reduce u, then v, then u again? Once u is a value, it can't step, so no. But interleaving: could we reduce v while u is not a value? No (ST_App2 requires value u). Could we reduce u after reducing v? Only if u is not a value, but to reduce v we needed u to be a value. Contradiction. So the sequence has the form:
  app u v -->* app u0 v (u -->* u0) [maybe u0 not a value], 
  then if u0 is a value: app u0 v -->* app u0 v0 (v -->* v0), 
  then if u0 = abs and v0 value: beta → open body v0,
  and if u0 is not a value: stuck (or rather, the term app u0 v is a normal form if u0 normal) — wait, u0 is reached by u -->* u0 where u0 might not be normal. Hmm, the sequence app u v -->* z: the reduction of u may stop at u0 which could still be reducible? No—if app u v -->* app u0 v and then more steps, those steps are either ST_App1 (u0 steps) or ST_App2 (needs value u0) or beta (needs u0 abs). So u0 could be a non-value non-normal term and the sequence continues with ST_App1. So for a *maximal* sequence ending at a value, the last step must be beta. Let's define the analysis:

Lemma `app_multi_value_inv`: If `app u v -->* z` and `value z`, then there exist u0 body v0 such that:
  u -->* tm_abs T body, v -->* v0, value v0, z = open body v0, locally_closed (tm_abs T body).
Additionally we might need the intermediate facts.

Proof: induction on the multi derivation.
- multi_refl: app u v = z value — impossible (values are abs/true/false; app isn't).
- multi_step: app u v --> t1 and t1 -->* z. Case analysis on `app u v --> t1`:
  - ST_AppAbs: u = abs T body, value v, t1 = open body v. Then open body v -->* z with value z. Need: exists u0 body' v0, u -->* abs ..., v -->* v0, value v0, z = open body' v0. Take u0 = u (refl), body' = body, v0 = v (refl), value v ✓, z = open body v... but the IH gives z = open body'' v0 for some reducts; hmm, we need to relate to u and v. In this case, the first step is beta and then open body v -->* z. We can just take u0 = u, v0 = v, and z = ... wait the conclusion requires z = open body' v0 where u -->* abs T body' and v -->* v0. Take body' = body, v0 = v: need z = open body v, but z is a reduct of open body v, not necessarily equal. Hmm, so the statement needs refinement: z = open body v0 for some v0 with v -->* v0? Let's reconsider.

Maybe a better statement: if `app u v -->* z` and value z, then there exist T body v0 with `u -->* tm_abs T body`, `v -->* v0`, `value v0`, and `open body v0 -->* z`. But z is a value, and open body v0 -->* z. Then from R T2 (open body v0) we get V T2 z by the value-reduct property ✓.

Hmm, but if z is a value and open body v0 -->* z, we don't need z = open body v0 exactly. Good, let's state:

`app_multi_value_inv`: `app u v -->* z -> value z -> exists T body v0, u -->* tm_abs T body /\ v -->* v0 /\ value v0 /\ open body v0 -->* z`.

Proof by induction on the multi derivation:
- refl: app u v value z, contradiction.
- step: app u v --> t1, t1 -->* z.
  - ST_AppAbs: u = abs T body, value v, t1 = open body v. Then open body v -->* z. Take T, body, v0 = v: u -->* u refl ✓, v -->* v refl ✓, value v ✓, open body v -->* z ✓. Done.
  - ST_App2: u value, v --> v', t1 = app u v'. IH: exists T body v0, u -->* abs T body, v' -->* v0, value v0, open body v0 -->* z. Then v -->* v0 via step+multi ✓.
  - ST_App1: u --> u', lc v, t1 = app u' v. IH: exists T body v0, u' -->* abs T body, v -->* v0, value v0, open body v0 -->* z. Then u -->* abs ✓.
✓ Great.

Also, we need the SN part: for the SN of `app u v`, we use the general app SN lemma with compat: forall u' v', u -->* u' -> value u' -> v -->* v' -> value v' -> SN (open body v') where u' = abs T body. From R u and R v we derive V (T1->T2) u' and V T1 v', then R T2 (app u' v'), whose SN gives SN (app u' v'), and app u' v' --> open body v' gives SN (open body v') ✓.

Wait, but the compat in the SN lemma I formulated was `u -->* tm_abs T body -> value v' -> v -->* v' -> SN (open body v')`. Note it doesn't require value u' since u' is an abs which is a value ✓.

Now, is the SN lemma's assumption `lc v` needed? Yes for ST_App1. R T1 v gives lc v ✓.

Lemma `has_type_lc : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> locally_closed t`.

Hmm, wait: is the lemma `lc_at k (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) -> lc_at (S k) t` actually true in general? Let's prove it by induction on t (generalizing k):
- t = bvar i: open_rec k x (bvar i) = if i =? k then fvar x else bvar i. lc_at k of the result.
  - If i = k: result fvar x, lc_at k ✓ trivially; need lc_at (S k) (bvar k): k < S k ✓.
  - If i ≠ k: result bvar i, lc_at k (bvar i) means i < k, so i < S k ✓.

- true/false ✓.

We can also use a simpler approach: `exists x, ~ In x L` can be proven by induction on L: for L = [], pick 0; for L = a :: L', get x fresh for L' by IH, then pick max (S x) (S a) or something. Lemma fresh_atom : forall L : list atom, exists x, ~ In x L.

- exists 0; intros [].

Standard approach: use the max of the list: x = 1 + fold_right max 0 L. Then for any y ∈ L, y <= fold_right max 0 L < x, so y ≠ x ✓. Prove `In y L -> y <= fold_right max 0 L` by induction on L. Now the fundamental theorem's environment. Let's define:

```
Definition subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...
Definition env_rel (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  (forall x, locally_closed (sigma x)) /\
  (forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> R T (sigma x)).
```

Fundamental theorem:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, env_rel Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t).
```
Proof by induction on the has_type derivation.

- T_Var: Gamma x = Some T. subst sigma (fvar x) = sigma x. Apply env_rel's second component ✓.

- T_Abs: 
  Goal: R (T1 -> T2) (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)).
  - lc (tm_abs T1 (subst sigma t1)): need lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1). From has_type_lc on the abs? Wait — we have `has_type Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (T1->T2)`; by has_type_lc, lc_at 0 (abs T1 t1), i.e., lc_at 1 t1. Then lc_at 1 (subst sigma t1) by the subst-lc lemma with forall x, lc (sigma x) ✓.
  - SN: abs ✓.
  - value reducts: v = abs (by value_multi_eq). Need V (T1->T2) (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)): 
    - value ✓ (needs lc (abs ...) which we just proved).
    - forall w, V T1 w -> R T2 (app (abs T1 (subst sigma t1)) w).
      Given V T1 w. Let body := subst sigma t1.
      Need R T2 (app (abs T1 body) w):
      - lc: lc abs ✓, lc w from V T1 w (V implies value implies lc; V T1 w gives value w ✓).
      - SN (app (abs T1 body) w): Since w is a value and abs is lc+value, the only step is beta → open body w. Need SN (open body w). From R T2 (open body w) (which we'll establish) ✓.
      - value reducts z: app abs w -->* z with value z. First step is beta (abs value, w value) → open body w; so open body w -->* z; R T2 (open body w) gives V T2 z ✓.
      
      Now, R T2 (open body w): apply the IH to the body typing premise. We need to choose a fresh x ∉ L ∪ fv(t1), then:
        Hbody : has_type (x |-> T1; Gamma) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2 (from the T_Abs premise).
        IH at this typing with sigma' := extend x w sigma: need env_rel (x|->T1;Gamma) sigma':
          - forall y, lc (sigma' y): for y = x, sigma' x = w, lc w ✓; else sigma y ✓.
          - forall y S, (x|->T1;Gamma) y = Some S -> R S (sigma' y): 
            y = x: S = T1 and sigma' x = w; need R T1 w from V T1 w ✓.
            y ≠ x: Gamma y = Some S and sigma' y = sigma y; env_rel_sigma gives R S (sigma y) ✓.
        IH gives R T2 (subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x))).
        Then rewrite: subst sigma' (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) = open_rec 0 w (subst sigma t1) = open body w, using x ∉ fv t1 ✓.
        So R T2 (open body w) ✓.
        
- T_App: 
  Goal: R T2 (subst sigma (app t1 t2)) = R T2 (app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)).
  Let u := subst sigma t1, v := subst sigma t2.
  IH1: R (T1->T2) u. IH2: R T1 v.
  Need R T2 (app u v). This is the "application closure" lemma we discussed:
  
  Lemma `R_app : forall T1 T2 u v, R (T1->T2) u -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app u v)`.
  Proof:
  - lc: R_lc u, R_lc v → lc (app u v) ✓.
  - SN: apply the general SN-app lemma with compat:
      compat: forall (T : ty) (body : tm) (v' : tm), u -->* tm_abs T body -> value v' -> v -->* v' -> SN (open body v').
      Given such: V (T1->T2) (abs T body) from R (T1->T2) u and the value reduct (abs T body): R gives forall z, u -->* z -> value z -> V (T1->T2) z; take z = abs T body ✓ (value abs needs lc: value_lc of abs gives lc ✓ — wait, `value (tm_abs T body)` requires lc; we need to show value (abs T body) to apply R's value-reduct property. Hmm, do we know lc (abs T body) from `u -->* abs T body`? We have lc u (from R u) and step preserves lc → lc (abs T body) ✓. Then value (abs T body) ✓ using lc_abs constructor).
      V T1 v' from R T1 v and v -->* v' value ✓.
      Then V (T1->T2) (abs T body) gives (forall w, V T1 w -> R T2 (app (abs T body) w)); with w = v': R T2 (app (abs T body) v'). Then SN (app (abs T body) v') from R_sn. And app (abs T body) v' --> open body v' (ST_AppAbs: lc abs ✓ from value_lc, value v' ✓). So SN (open body v') ✓ by SN inversion.
    Then the SN-app lemma gives SN (app u v) with lc v ✓.
  - value reducts z: given app u v -->* z, value z. Use `app_multi_value_inv` to get T body v' with u -->* abs T body, v -->* v', value v', open body v' -->* z. As above, V (T1->T2) (abs T body) and V T1 v' → R T2 (app (abs T body) v') → forward closure (or: R gives value-reducts property) with app (abs T body) v' --> open body v' -->* z. So z ∈ V T2 ✓. Note: R T2 (app abs v') has the "all value reducts" property: any z with app abs v' -->* z and value z is in V T2 ✓. And app abs v' --> open body v' -->* z ✓.

Good. Note that in `R_app` we need `R` to be closed under multi-step forward. Let's prove `R_multi : R T t -> t -->* t' -> R T t'`. This is easy with the definition:
- lc: multi preserves lc (induction using step_preserves_lc) ✓.
- SN: SN t → t -->* t' → SN t'. Prove by induction on multi using SN inversion ✓.
- value reducts: t' -->* z → t -->* z ✓ by multi_trans.
Need `multi_trans : t1 -->* t2 -> t2 -->* t3 -> t1 -->* t3` ✓.

Also R needs: `R_lc`, `R_sn` (trivial projections via T induction? Since R is a Fixpoint with different shapes per T, projection needs induction on T. Let's define R to have a uniform shape? Hmm. If R is defined by Fixpoint with match on T returning different conjunctions, then `R T t -> lc t` requires induction on T (for the arrow case it's a direct projection). Let's just prove by induction on T. Similarly R_sn.

But there's a subtlety: R's definition at arrow includes `forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app t v)`. When we do induction on T to prove R_sn, the arrow case is a pure projection; no recursive use needed. Fine.

And R_lc: arrow case projection. Fine.

Now V. Let's define V as a Fixpoint:
```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => value t /\ (forall v, V T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.
```
And R:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
  (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> V T v).
```
But R must be defined simultaneously with V since V references R... or define R first requiring V, but R's body references V T with the same T. Mutual recursion where R calls V at same T and V calls R at T2 (subterm). Coq's guard checker for mutual fixpoints: calls between different functions must be on structurally smaller arguments? Let's recall: For mutually recursive fixpoints, Coq requires that recursive calls (to any of the functions in the block) are on structurally smaller arguments of the same function's argument? Hmm, the guard condition for mutual fixpoints: "the recursive calls must be made on structurally smaller arguments". Specifically, when f calls g, the argument passed to g must be a subterm of the argument of f? I think Coq's guard condition tracks the "recursive argument" per function; mutual calls must decrease. `R T` calls `V T` on the same T → not decreasing → rejected.

Workaround: define V in terms of R without mutual recursion by inlining. Since V T t at Bool is `t = true ∨ t = false`, and at arrow is `value t ∧ forall v, V T1 v -> R T2 (app t v)`. We can define a single Fixpoint `V` and define R in terms of V, where V's arrow clause refers to... R T2, which refers to V T2. So V needs R at subterms, R needs V at the same type. We could define `R` and `V` such that both are a single Fixpoint on T returning a pair of predicates? E.g., define a single function `RV T : (tm -> Prop) * (tm -> Prop)`; then define R and V as projections. Then the Fixpoint's recursive calls are all on subterms ✓. Let's do that, or better:

Alternative: define R with the value-reduct property expressed in terms of R itself, avoiding V:
```
R T t := lc t /\ SN t /\ forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V T v
```
where V T v could be defined as `value v /\ R T v`? Let's revisit: is `V T v := value v /\ R T v` workable? For T = Bool: V Bool v = value v ∧ lc v ∧ SN v ∧ all-value-reducts. Since v is a value, all value reducts of v = {v}, so V Bool v says: v value, lc, SN, and V Bool v... circular. Hmm, the definition would be circular in the sense that the inner V at the same T. Not structurally decreasing either.

OK, let's use a single mutual-ish Fixpoint returning a pair:
```
Fixpoint RV (T : ty) : (tm -> Prop) * (tm -> Prop) :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => (fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
                          (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false))),
                (fun v => v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false))
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      (fun t => locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
                 (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> 
                    (value v /\ (forall w, (snd (RV T1)) w -> (fst (RV T2)) (tm_app v w))))),
      (fun v => value v /\ (forall w, (snd (RV T1)) w -> (fst (RV T2)) (tm_app v w)))
  end.
Definition R T := fst (RV T).
Definition V T := snd (RV T).
```
This is structurally recursive ✓. But then all the proofs need to unfold RV/R/V carefully. Since R T = fst (RV T), and V T = snd (RV T), we can prove unfolding lemmas:
```
Lemma R_bool_unfold : forall t, R Ty_Bool t = (locally_closed t /\ SN t /\ (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (v = true \/ v = false)))...
```
Ugly but manageable. Alternatively, since Coq supports mutual Fixpoints with the guard condition, maybe we can arrange V to be defined first (as a Fixpoint in T) where V's arrow case uses R at T2, and R is defined *after* V as a plain Definition using V. Then V's definition mentions R which isn't defined yet — circular at the level of definitions, not allowed.

Alternative: Define R independently without V:
R T t := lc t ∧ SN t ∧ (forall v, t -->* v -> value v -> V T v), where V is defined *using R* — circular.

Hmm, what if we inline V into R? Define R as a Fixpoint where the value-reduct property is expressed with an inline match on T:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->
      (forall w, R T1 w -> value w -> R T2 (tm_app v w))
  end.
```
Note: I inlined V (T1->T2) v as `forall w, R T1 w -> value w -> R T2 (app v w)` — hmm, careful with the V definition: V (T1->T2) v = value v ∧ forall w, V T1 w -> R T2 (app v w). If I replace V T1 w with `R T1 w ∧ value w`, that's an equivalent but different formulation; and since in R's definition we already require `value v` as a hypothesis, we don't need it inside.

Wait, but there's a subtlety: is V T1 w equivalent to R T1 w ∧ value w? Let's check: R T1 w = lc w ∧ SN w ∧ (all value reducts of w in V T1). V T1 w = value w ∧ ... For T1 = Bool: V Bool w = w = true ∨ w = false. R Bool w ∧ value w = lc ∧ SN ∧ (all value reducts are true/false) ∧ value w. Since w is a value, multi w w' with value w' implies w' = w, so the value-reduct clause reduces to V Bool w = (w = true ∨ w = false). So R Bool w ∧ value w ↔ value w ∧ (w = true ∨ w = false) ↔ w = true ∨ w = false (since true/false are values) = V Bool w. So they're equivalent for Bool ✓.
For arrow: R (T1->T2) w ∧ value w = lc w ∧ SN w ∧ (all value reducts v of w are in V (T1->T2)) ∧ value w. Since value w, value reducts of w = {w} (as value_multi_eq gives v = w). So it's lc w ∧ SN w ∧ V (T1->T2) w ∧ value w. Now, does V imply lc ∧ SN? V (T1->T2) w = value w ∧ forall z, V T1 z -> R T2 (app w z). lc w from value_lc ✓; SN w from value no-step ✓. So R ∧ value ↔ V ∧ value ↔ V. So yes, `V T v ↔ R T v ∧ value v`. 

So we can define R for all T uniformly in terms of R at subterms, and use `value v ∧ R T v` as V. But careful: R's definition at arrow references `R T1 w` and `R T2 (app v w)`, and at Bool no subterm reference — this is a plain Fixpoint on T ✓. Then prove the equivalence `V_iff : (R T v ∧ value v) ↔ V T v` by induction on T. Hmm, but the definition of R at arrow uses `R T1 w` for arbitrary w, including non-values; while V's clause restricts to V T1 w (which implies R T1 w and value w). The equivalence proof needs care:

Define:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => (forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false))
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => (forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->
       (forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (tm_app v w)))
  end.
```
Hmm, wait: at arrow, I need for value-reducts v: V (T1->T2) v, which is `value v ∧ forall w, V T1 w -> R T2 (app v w)`. I've already got `value v` as a hypothesis in the outer forall. And V T1 w ↔ value w ∧ R T1 w. So the clause `forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (app v w)` ✓ matches.

At Bool: V Bool v = v = true ∨ v = false ✓.

So define V T v := value v ∧ R T v, and prove `V_T_iff : value v -> (V T v ↔ ...)` hmm; actually we can just work with `value v /\ R T v` everywhere instead of V, proving the needed lemmas directly:
- If `value v` and `R (T1->T2) v`, then for all w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (app v w): from R's arrow clause, take the value reduct v itself (v -->* v and value v) ✓. This works without V.
- If `value v` and `R T1 v`, get R of value reducts via R_multi ✓.

So maybe we don't need V at all! Let's restate the T_Abs case:
Need R (T1->T2) (abs T1 body):
- lc ✓, SN ✓.
- arrow clause: forall v, abs -->* v -> value v -> forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (app v w).
  Given abs -->* v, value v: v = abs (value_multi_eq). Given w, value w, R T1 w. Need R T2 (app (abs T1 body) w).
  - lc ✓
  - SN: only beta step → open body w, need SN (open body w). From R T2 (open body w) via the fundamental theorem at the body with sigma' mapping x to w (need R T1 w ✓ and lc w from value ✓).
  - arrow clause of R T2 (if T2 arrow): forall z, app abs w -->* z -> value z -> forall y, value y -> R T2a y -> R T2b (app z y).
    Hmm! This is the "value reducts" clause where the reduct z is a value. Since app abs w is not a value, z must come from a step. First step is beta (abs value, w value) → open body w -->* z. From R T2 (open body w), we get the arrow clause at z ✓ (since open body w -->* z, value z, then forall y ... R T2b (app z y)) ✓.
    
So the R definition's third component at arrow type is: forall v (value reduct), value v -> forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (app v w). Let me re-derive the T_App case with this definition:
R_app: R (T1->T2) u -> R T1 v -> R T2 (app u v).
- lc ✓, SN via app lemma ✓.
- clause at T2:
  - If T2 = Bool: forall z, app u v -->* z -> value z -> z = true ∨ z = false. From app_multi_value_inv: u -->* abs T body, v -->* v', value v', open body v' -->* z. R u gives (value reduct abs T body): value (abs T body) [needs lc: lc u + lc preserved ✓] and forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (app abs w). R v gives value reduct v': value v' and R T1 v' (via R_multi). So R T2 (app (abs T body) v'). Then R T2's Bool clause applied to z' where app abs v' -->* z: first beta step then open body v' -->* z; so app abs v' -->* z and value z → z = true ∨ z = false ✓. Wait, careful: R T2 = R Bool (app abs v') = lc ∧ SN ∧ (forall z, app abs v' -->* z -> value z -> z = true ∨ z = false). Yes! Since z is reachable ✓. So z = true ∨ z = false ✓.
  - If T2 = T2a -> T2b: forall z, app u v -->* z -> value z -> forall y, value y -> R T2a y -> R T2b (app z y). Given such z, y. From app_multi_value_inv: z is reached via u -->* abs T body, v -->* v', value v', and open body v' -->* z. As above, R T2 (app abs v') and hence (arrow clause) for value reduct z: forall y, value y -> R T2a y -> R T2b (app z y), giving R T2b (app z y) ✓.

So the single Fixpoint R works and no V needed. 

Let's write the definition concretely:

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  strongly_normalizing t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool =>
      forall v, multi step t v -> value v -> (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      forall v, multi step t v -> value v ->
        forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (tm_app v w)
  end.
```

Hmm, note the match is on T returning Prop; with T in the fixpoint's decreasing argument position ✓. R is called at T1 and T2 in the arrow branch — structurally smaller ✓. But careful: R T1 appears inside the match on T which is fine.

However, there's a subtlety: in the Bool branch, `multi step t v` uses `multi` from the task ✓.

Wait, one potential issue: the arrow clause quantifies over `w` with `R T1 w` — R T1 is a recursive call on T1, which is a subterm of T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2 ✓.

Now let's double-check the T_Abs case: we need the "value reducts of abs are abs" lemma and the R clause. In the arrow branch, we need for v with abs -->* v and value v: `forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (app v w)`. Since v = abs T1 body (from value_multi_eq + inversion: abs -->* v with value v means v = abs T1 body). Then app v w = app (abs T1 body) w ✓.

Hmm, careful: value_multi_eq says value t -> t -->* t' -> t' = t. Applied with t = abs T1 body (value requires lc ✓): v = abs T1 body ✓.

At Bool branch for R (abs ...): forall v, abs -->* v -> value v -> v = true ∨ v = false. Since v = abs, and abs ≠ true, abs ≠ false → contradiction. So we need to handle: abs -->* v and value v implies v = abs, but then the goal `abs = true ∨ abs = false` is false! Wait, that means R Bool (abs T1 body) would be FALSE. But R Bool t should only be needed for well-typed terms of type Bool, which are never abstractions. Indeed, the T_Abs case only needs R (T1->T2) (abs...), not R Bool. Good. But is R's definition sensible? For a term of Bool type, the value reducts must be true/false. An abs of Bool type is ill-typed and R Bool (abs) = false, which is fine.

But hold on: the clause at Bool doesn't require the term itself to be true/false, only its value reducts. And a term with no value reducts (stuck or diverging) satisfies it vacuously (given SN). E.g., `app true true` has no value reducts → R Bool holds if lc and SN ✓. Good.

Now the T_Abs case requires R (T1->T2) (abs T1 body) which requires lc, SN, and the arrow clause. ✓ All fine since we never need value reducts of the abs to be true/false.

Now let's carefully verify R_app's SN part again with this R.

We need a lemma:

**sn_app**: forall u v, locally_closed u -> locally_closed v -> SN u -> SN v ->
   (forall T body v', u -->* tm_abs T body -> v -->* v' -> value v' -> strongly_normalizing (open body v')) ->
   strongly_normalizing (tm_app u v).

Hmm, in the "compat" we don't need value (abs T body) since it's automatic. But we do need lc (abs T body) to apply ST_AppAbs inside the SN proof? Let's see: In the inner lemma (value u), case ST_AppAbs: the rule requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T t)`. We have `value u` and u = abs T body, so value_lc gives lc ✓. Good, so we can get it from value u.

Let me restate the proof structure with `value u`:

Inner lemma (I): forall u, value u -> forall v, locally_closed v -> SN v ->
  (forall T body v', u -->* tm_abs T body -> v -->* v' -> value v' -> SN (open body v')) ->
  SN (app u v).

Hmm, note that for a value u, u -->* abs T body with u = abs T body' possibly. Actually u value means u is abs/true/false. If u = abs, then u -->* abs T body implies abs T body = u. In the inner lemma's ST_AppAbs case, u must be an abs (since ST_AppAbs's left side is tm_app (tm_abs T t) v). So u = abs T0 body0 and the step gives open body0 v; compat with u -->* u refl and v -->* v refl, value v gives SN (open body0 v) ✓.

Proof of (I) by induction on SN v (Acc induction):
Take u value, v with SN v, hypotheses. Show SN (app u v) via SN_intro: for any t' with app u v --> t', show SN t'.
- ST_AppAbs: u = abs T body (from inversion of value u? Careful: value u could be true/false; but the rule ST_AppAbs requires the app's first component to be tm_abs T t, so if app u v = app (abs T t) v then u = abs T t by injectivity of tm_app; so this case applies only when u is an abs). Result open t v: compat u -->* u (refl) and v -->* v (refl) with value v ✓.
- ST_App2: value u ✓ (hyp), v --> v'; result app u v'. Apply IH to v' (SN v' from SN v inversion) with lc v' (from lc v and step preserves lc) and compat v' (derived from compat v by extending multi: v' -->* v'' then v -->* v'' ✓) ✓.
- ST_App1: requires u --> u'; but value u → no step (value_no_step) ✓.

Outer lemma (II): forall u, locally_closed u -> SN u -> forall v, locally_closed v -> SN v -> compat u v -> SN (app u v).
Proof by induction on SN u (Acc):
For any t' with app u v --> t':
- ST_App1: u --> u', result app u' v. Apply IH to u' (SN u' from SN u inversion), with lc u' (from lc u and step: lc preservation forward? Here we have u --> u' and lc u; need lc u'. step_lc_fwd ✓), lc v, SN v, and compat u' v (derived: u' -->* abs ... from u -->* ... ✓).
- ST_App2: value u, v --> v', result app u v'. Apply inner lemma (I) at u: value u, need lc v' ✓, SN v' ✓, compat u v' ✓.
- ST_AppAbs: needs lc (tm_abs T t) and value v: result open t v. Compat gives SN (open t v) using u -->* u refl (u = abs T t) and v -->* v refl and value v ✓.
✓

Wait, in case ST_AppAbs of the outer lemma, the compat hypothesis is stated with `u -->* tm_abs T body`, and u = tm_abs T t, so u -->* u ✓.

Also, in case ST_App1, we need `locally_closed v` (the rule's premise), which we have.

So `sn_app` holds. But hold on: the induction on SN u — the statement of (II) is `forall v, lc v -> SN v -> compat u v -> SN (app u v)`, and we do Acc induction on SN u. In Coq: `induction 1` where the Acc is `strongly_normalizing u`. Since strongly_normalizing is defined with the same shape as Acc, we can do `induction Hu` giving IH: forall y, u --> y -> (forall v, lc v -> SN v -> compat y v -> SN (app y v)). Hmm, but compat y v must be provided by the caller — in the ST_App1 case, we derive compat u' v from compat u v ✓.

Careful: the Acc induction principle for `strongly_normalizing` is the induction scheme generated by Coq for the inductive `strongly_normalizing`, whose IH is over immediate reducts ✓.

OK. Now, the SN of the abstraction: `SN (tm_abs T body)`. Prove lemma `SN_abs : forall T body, strongly_normalizing (tm_abs T body)`: apply SN_intro; intros t' Hstep; inversion Hstep — there's no step rule for abs, so all cases are impossible via the equality app = abs. Coq should solve it with `inversion Hstep`. Hmm, actually `step` has constructors whose conclusions are `tm_app ... --> ...`; to derive `tm_abs T body --> t'`, inversion will fail to unify `tm_app` with `tm_abs`, closing the goal ✓. Actually, `inversion Hstep` should solve it.

Similarly `SN_true`, `SN_false` by inversion.

And `value_no_step : value t -> t --> t' -> False`: destruct value; inversion Hstep (no constructor matches: for abs, the step conclusion is app; for true/false similarly) ✓.

`value_lc : value t -> locally_closed t`: destruct: v_abs gives lc ✓; v_true/v_false → lc_true/lc_false ✓.

Now, key remaining pieces:
1. Substitution definition and lemmas:
   - `subst_lc : lc_at k t -> (forall x, lc (sigma x)) -> lc_at k (subst sigma t)`.
   - `subst_open : x ∉ fv t -> subst (extend x u sigma) (open_rec k (fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst sigma t)`. Hmm, need to state carefully. Actually, we need it for k=0 with open. Let's prove a general version:
     `forall t k sigma', (forall y, y ∈ fv t -> sigma' y = sigma y) -> (forall y, ... hmm)`.
     Simplest: prove for the specific extension:
     `subst (extend x u sigma) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst sigma t)` when `~ In x (fv t)` and ... wait, there's another subtlety: what if t contains bvar indices ≥ k under binders? The opening and substitution should commute fine.
     
     Let's try to prove it by induction on t (generalizing k):
     - bvar i: open_rec k (fvar x) (bvar i) = if i =? k then fvar x else bvar i.
       - If i = k: LHS = subst sigma' (fvar x) = sigma' x = u. RHS = open_rec k u (subst sigma (bvar i)) = open_rec k u (bvar i) = u (since i = k) ✓.
       - Else: LHS = subst sigma' (bvar i) = bvar i. RHS = open_rec k u (bvar i) = bvar i (since i ≠ k) ✓.
     - fvar y: LHS = subst sigma' (fvar y) = sigma' y. RHS = open_rec k u (subst sigma (fvar y)) = open_rec k u (sigma y).
       Hmm! RHS = open_rec k u (sigma y) which is not sigma y in general — it opens bvar k in sigma y! Wait, that's a mismatch. Because in the RHS, `subst sigma t` where t = fvar y gives sigma y, and then `open_rec k u` is applied to it, replacing bvar k inside sigma y. But in the LHS, `open_rec k (fvar x) (fvar y)` = fvar y, then subst sigma' gives sigma' y = sigma y (since y ≠ x as x ∉ fv t... actually y is free in t = fvar y so x ∉ fv t means x ≠ y). So LHS = sigma y, RHS = open_rec k u (sigma y). These differ if sigma y contains a free bvar k!
       
       Hmm! So the lemma as stated is FALSE. The issue: the RHS opens the substituted term, and the substituted values (sigma y) may contain free bvars that get captured. In our use, sigma maps to *locally closed* terms, so bvar k would not occur... but open_rec k u (sigma y) with k=0 and sigma y locally closed: open_rec 0 u (sigma y) = sigma y since no bvar 0 occurs ✓. Wait, open_rec replaces bvar 0 occurrences; if sigma y is lc_at 0, no bvar 0 occurs... hmm, but under binders inside sigma y, open_rec 0 looks for bvar 0 there too? open_rec 0 u (abs T t') = abs T (open_rec 1 u t') — inside one binder, indices shift, so bvar 0 inside a binder is a different variable. Hmm: if sigma y is locally closed (lc_at 0), then inside a binder at depth 1, all bvars are < 1, i.e., bvar 0 may occur — that's the bound variable! And open_rec 0 at the top level: under a binder, it replaces bvar 1, not bvar 0, so bound bvars are untouched. So `lc (sigma y) -> open_rec 0 u (sigma y) = sigma y` should hold. Let's prove: `open_rec k u t = t` if `lc_at k t`. Prove by induction on t: bvar i with i < k: i ≠ k ✓ (since i < k); abs: lc_at k of abs means lc_at (S k) body, IH ✓. Great: `open_lc : lc_at k t -> open_rec k u t = t`.
       
       So the substitution-open lemma needs `forall y, lc (sigma y)` (locally closed substitution values) so that opening doesn't affect them. In our environment relation, we have `forall x, lc (sigma x)` ✓. 
       
       So state: `subst_open` with hypotheses: `~ In x (fv t)`, `forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)`, and `locally_closed u`? For the bvar case with i = k: RHS = open_rec k u (subst sigma (bvar i)) = open_rec k u (bvar k) = u ✓ regardless of u. Hmm, and for fvar y: RHS = open_rec k u (sigma y) = sigma y using lc sigma y ✓ (need at level k: lc_at k (sigma y) from lc_at 0 and monotonicity ✓). For app/abs: structural ✓.
       
       So: `subst_open : forall t k x u sigma, ~ In x (fv t) -> (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) -> subst (extend x u sigma) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst sigma t)`.
       
       Hmm wait, there's still the `extend` definition: `extend x u sigma := fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y`. And `subst sigma' (tm_fvar x) = sigma' x = u` (eqb x x = true) ✓.

   Also for the lc of subst sigma' (open t (fvar x)): we need lc_at 0 (open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1) from lc_at 1 t1 (the lc-open lemma forward direction) ✓: `lc_open : lc_at (S k) t -> lc_at k u -> lc_at k (open_rec k u t)` — here u = fvar x is lc ✓.

2. Freshness: `fv : tm -> list atom`:
   ```
   Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
     match t with
     | tm_bvar _ => nil
     | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
     | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
     | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
     | tm_true | tm_false => nil
     end.
   ```
   And `fresh : list atom -> atom` with `fresh_notin : ~ In (fresh L) L`.
   
   In the T_Abs case, we need x fresh w.r.t. L and fv t1. So use `fresh (L ++ fv t1)`.

3. The fundamental theorem as above.

4. Final theorem: `normalization : forall t T, empty |-- t \in T -> SN t`.
   Apply fundamental with sigma := fun _ => tm_true (which is lc everywhere) and env_rel empty sigma (the second component is vacuous since empty x = None ≠ Some T) — careful: `empty x = None`, so `empty x = Some T` is impossible ✓. Then subst sigma t: hmm, we get R T (subst sigma t), not R T t. We need SN t. Since t is closed (well-typed at empty context), subst sigma t = t! Need a lemma: if t has no free fvars (i.e., all fvars in t are in Gamma's domain... for empty context, t has no fvars), then subst sigma t = t.
   
   Prove: `has_type empty t T -> forall x, In x (fv t) -> False`? Or more generally, `has_type Gamma t T -> In x (fv t) -> exists S, Gamma x = Some S`. For empty, that gives False. Let's prove the general free-variable lemma:
   `has_type_free : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall x, In x (fv t) -> exists S, Gamma x = Some S`.
   Proof by induction on the typing derivation:
   - T_Var: Gamma x = Some T; fv (fvar y) contains y; x = y → exists T ✓.
   - T_Abs: fv (abs T1 t1) = fv t1. Given x ∈ fv t1. Hmm, we need to relate x ∈ fv t1 with x ∈ fv (open t1 (fvar x0)) for a fresh x0. Standard lemma: `fv (open_rec k (fvar y) t) = ...` hmm. Actually, `fv (open t1 (fvar x0)) = fv t1 ++ [x0]`? Opening replaces bvar 0 with fvar x0, adding x0 to the free vars (and those under binders). So x ∈ fv t1 implies x ∈ fv (open t1 (fvar x0)) ✓. Need lemma `fv_open : In x (fv t) -> In x (fv (open_rec k (tm_fvar y) t))` for any k, y. Prove by induction on t:
     - bvar i: if i = k → fv = [y], and x ∈ fv (bvar i) = [] → contradiction. else fv = [] → contradiction.
     - fvar z: x = z, and fv (fvar z) = [z] ✓.
     - app ✓, abs ✓ (IH).
     - true/false: contradiction.
   Then IH on the premise typing with the fresh x0 gives exists S, (x0 |-> T1; Gamma) x = Some S. If x = x0 then S = T1 (exists); else Gamma x = Some S ✓.
   
   Hmm, but rather than this, maybe simpler: prove `lc_fv`? No, lc doesn't imply absence of fvars.

   Alternatively: show that `subst sigma t = t` when all fvars of t are "not in the domain of empty" — but sigma maps everything to true, so we need t to have no fvars at all. Let's prove: `has_type empty t T -> fv t = nil`? Hmm, can't easily prove equality; the `exists S, empty x = Some S` route gives False directly.

   Let's set up: `has_type_fv : has_type Gamma t T -> forall x, In x (fv t) -> Gamma x <> None`? Hmm, contexts are functions to option; "Gamma x ≠ None" means exists S, Gamma x = Some S. Since option ty is decidable, `Gamma x <> None -> exists S, Gamma x = Some S` holds (destruct (Gamma x)) ✓. So we can just prove `Gamma x <> None`.

   Then `subst_id : (forall x, In x (fv t) -> sigma x = t... )`. Hmm, we want subst sigma t = t when for all x ∈ fv t, sigma x = tm_fvar x. Since empty maps to None, sigma can be arbitrary on the (nonexistent) fvars. So define sigma := fun x => tm_fvar x (identity substitution)! Then env_rel empty sigma: forall x, lc (fvar x) ✓; forall x S, empty x = Some S -> R S (fvar x) — vacuous ✓. Then fundamental gives R T (subst identity t) = R T t (need subst_id: subst (fun x => fvar x) t = t, which is true by induction on t, no freshness needed ✓). Then R_sn gives SN t ✓.

   So use sigma := fun x => tm_fvar x. That's cleaner: no free-variable lemma needed for subst identity! But we DO need subst identity: `subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t` — prove by induction on t ✓ trivial.

   Then the final theorem: apply fundamental with sigma = fvar; env_rel empty (fun x => fvar x) ✓ vacuous second component; get R T (subst sigma t) = R T t; R_sn → SN t ✓. No need for has_type_fv at all! 

Now, about T_Abs's premise and the IH: `has_type (x |-> T1; Gamma) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` — but note the premise in the rule is for the *term* `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` — where `open` is defined as `open_rec 0 u t`. ✓.

Let me now write out the full proof plan in Rocq.

```coq
From Stdlib Require Import Arith.PeanoNat.
From Stdlib Require Import Lists.List.
From Stdlib Require Import Relations.Relation_Definitions.

Module STLCNormalizationNoneHardTask.

... given stuff ...

(** My additions *)

(* ---------- basic list freshness ---------- *)
Fixpoint max_list (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: L' => Nat.max x (max_list L')
  end.

Lemma max_list_spec : forall L x, In x L -> x <= max_list L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| a L' IH]; intros x Hin.
  - destruct Hin.
  - destruct Hin as [<- | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + eapply Nat.le_trans; [apply IH; eauto | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (max_list L).

Lemma fresh_notin : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. unfold fresh in Hin.
  pose proof (max_list_spec L _ Hin). lia.
Qed.

(* ---------- free variables ---------- *)
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom := ...

Lemma fv_open : forall t k y x, In x (fv t) -> In x (fv (open_rec k (tm_fvar y) t)).
...

(* ---------- substitution ---------- *)
Fixpoint subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm := ...

Definition extend (x : atom) (u : tm) (sigma : atom -> tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then u else sigma y.

Lemma subst_id : forall t, subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Lemma subst_open : forall t k x u sigma, ~ In x (fv t) -> (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->
   subst (extend x u sigma) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst sigma t).
Lemma subst_lc : forall sigma t k, lc_at k t -> (forall x, locally_closed (sigma x)) -> lc_at k (subst sigma t).
```

Wait, in subst_open, there's the issue of `sigma y` being opened by `open_rec k u (sigma y)`, requiring `lc_at k (sigma y)`. From `locally_closed (sigma y)` and monotonicity: `lc_at 0 (sigma y) -> lc_at k (sigma y)` ✓.

Also `open_lc : lc_at k t -> forall u, open_rec k u t = t`.

Let me be careful with the induction in subst_open: generalize over k (and maybe sigma, x, u fixed). Statement:
```
Lemma subst_open : forall t, ~ In x (fv t) -> ... 
```
but the k-generalization inside the induction matters for the abs case: open_rec k (fvar x) (abs T t1) = abs T (open_rec (S k) (fvar x) t1). The IH must be at S k. So prove:
```
Lemma subst_open_gen : forall t k x u sigma,
  ~ In x (fv t) -> (forall y, locally_closed (sigma y)) ->
  subst (extend x u sigma) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_rec k u (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  induction t; intros k x u sigma Hfresh Hlc; simpl.
  ...
```
Coq's `induction t` before introducing k might create issues; better `intros t; induction t; intros k x u sigma Hfresh Hlc`.

Cases:
- tm_bvar n: simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E. 
  + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E; subst n. simpl. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. — hmm, let's compute: LHS: subst sigma' (if k =? k then fvar x else bvar k)... Actually `open_rec k (tm_fvar x) (tm_bvar n)` reduces to `if Nat.eqb k n then tm_fvar x else tm_bvar n`. Then `subst sigma'` of that. With n = k: (fvar x) → sigma' x = (if x =? x then u else sigma x) = u. RHS: open_rec k u (subst sigma (bvar n)) = open_rec k u (bvar k) = u ✓.
  + n ≠ k: LHS = subst sigma' (bvar n) = bvar n. RHS = open_rec k u (bvar n) = bvar n ✓.
  Note the eqb in `open_rec` is `Nat.eqb k n` (k first), and in `extend` it's `Nat.eqb x y`.
- tm_fvar y: Need y ≠ x from freshness: Hfresh : ~ In x (fv (fvar y)) = ~ In x (y :: nil), so x ≠ y. LHS = sigma' y = if x =? y then u else sigma y = sigma y (since x ≠ y). RHS = open_rec k u (sigma y) = sigma y by open_lc with lc_at k (sigma y) ✓.
  Note: need `Nat.eqb x y = false`; from x ≠ y, `Nat.eqb_neq` ✓.
- tm_app t1 t2: simpl. Rewrite IH twice ✓. Freshness: In x (fv (app t1 t2)) = In x (fv t1 ++ fv t2); ~In → both ✓ (using `in_app_iff` / `app` lemmas).
- tm_abs T t1: simpl. Need IH at (S k). Freshness ✓ (fv same). Result: subst sigma' (abs T (open_rec (S k) (fvar x) t1)) = abs T (subst sigma' (open_rec (S k) ...)) = abs T (open_rec (S k) u (subst sigma t1)) = open_rec k u (abs T (subst sigma t1)) ✓.
- tm_true/false ✓.

`subst_lc`:
```
Lemma subst_lc : forall t k sigma, lc_at k t -> (forall x, locally_closed (sigma x)) -> lc_at k (subst sigma t).
Proof.
  induction t; intros k sigma Hlc Hsig; simpl; try constructor; try easy.
  - (* bvar *) constructor; apply Hlc.  -- Hlc : lc_at k (bvar n) means n < k, and subst = bvar n.
    Actually inversion Hlc gives n < k; constructor; assumption.
  - (* fvar *) apply Hsig.
  - (* app *) inversion Hlc as [| | k' t1 t2 H1 H2 | | |]; subst. constructor; [apply IHt1|apply IHt2]; auto.
  - (* abs *) inversion Hlc; subst. constructor. apply IHt; auto.
```
Careful with the inversion of lc_at: constructors have an explicit k parameter. Let me write it as `inversion Hlc; subst; ...`.

Also `lc_at_mono : lc_at k t -> k <= k' -> lc_at k' t`. Induction on lc_at or on t. Let's prove by induction on t generalizing k,k'. Actually easier by induction on the derivation Hlc.
```
Lemma lc_at_mono : forall k t, lc_at k t -> forall k', k <= k' -> lc_at k' t.
Proof. induction 1; intros k' Hle; try constructor; auto. 
  - (* bvar *) constructor; lia.
  - (* abs *) apply lc_abs. apply IHlc_at. lia.
Qed.
```

`open_lc : lc_at k t -> forall u, open_rec k u t = t`:
```
Lemma open_lc : forall t k, lc_at k t -> forall u, open_rec k u t = t.
Proof.
  induction 1; intros u; simpl; auto.
  - (* bvar *) rewrite Nat.eqb_neq by lia. reflexivity.
  - (* fvar *) reflexivity.
  - (* app *) rewrite IHlc_at1, IHlc_at2; reflexivity.
  - (* abs *) rewrite IHlc_at. reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Now R definition and lemmas.

```coq
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  locally_closed t /\
  strongly_normalizing t /\
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => forall v, multistep t v -> value v -> (v = tm_true \/ v = tm_false)
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => forall v, multistep t v -> value v ->
        (forall w, value w -> R T1 w -> R T2 (tm_app v w))
  end.
```
